Averaging multipliers on locally compact quantum groups Thanks: This work was supported by EPSRC grants EP/T03064X/1 and EP/T030992/1. Additionally, JK was partially supported by FWO grant 1246624N
Matthew Daws, Jacek Krajczok, Christian Voigt

Abstract
. We study an averaging procedure for completely bounded multipliers on a locally compact quantum group with respect to a compact quantum subgroup. As a consequence we show that central approximation properties of discrete quantum groups are equivalent to the corresponding approximation properties of their Drinfeld doubles. This is complemented by a discussion of the averaging of Fourier algebra elements. We compare the biinvariant Fourier algebra of the Drinfeld double of a discrete quantum group with the central Fourier algebra. In the unimodular case these are naturally identified, but we show by exhibiting a family of counter-examples that they differ in general.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46L67, Secondary 22D55, 43A07, 43A30

1.
Introduction

Approximation properties like amenability, weak amenability, the Haagerup property and the Haagerup-Kraus approximation property have been studied extensively in the setting of
locally compact quantum groups, see
[
6
]
for a survey. In comparison to the case of groups, an interesting new feature in the quantum setting is the interplay between
discrete quantum groups, their Drinfeld doubles, and the associated
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories. In particular, the central versions of amenability, the Haagerup property, weak amenability
and central property (T) for discrete quantum groups can be recast at the level of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories
[
42
]
, thus building a natural bridge to the study
of subfactors. Central approximation properties, in turn, are key to a range of fundamental results regarding the analytic structure of discrete quantum
groups
[
18
]
,
[
21
]
.

In this paper we show that, for all the approximation properties mentioned above, the central version of the property for a discrete quantum group
is equivalent to the corresponding property of its Drinfeld double. Special cases of these equivalences were known previously,
and our main point is that they actually hold in complete generality. This yields a succinct conceptual understanding of central approximation properties, and highlights the key role
played by the Drinfeld double.

We prove in fact a more general result. Namely, for a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
with a compact quantum subgroup
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{G}
,
we show that averaging elements of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
with respect to left and right translations by
\mathbb{K}

maps completely bounded multipliers of
\mathbb{G}
to
\mathbb{K}
-biinvariant completely bounded multipliers of
\mathbb{G}
.
The resulting averaging map is contractive and preserves complete positivity, the Fourier algebra, and other standard properties of multipliers.
In the special case that
\mathbb{G}=D({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
is the Drinfeld double of a discrete quantum group

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

and
\mathbb{K}=\widehat{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}}
, the
\mathbb{K}
-biinvariant function algebras
may be indentified with the corresponding algebras of central functions on

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

, and we show that the same holds for completely bounded multipliers.
This yields our result linking the approximation properties of the Drinfeld double
D({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
with the central approximation properties of

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

.

We also discuss a number of related questions. For unimodular discrete quantum groups there is a well-known averaging procedure sending completely bounded multipliers to central
completely bounded multipliers,
[
6
,
32
]
. We show that this procedure, which is different from the above averaging with respect to compact
quantum subgroups, maps the Fourier algebra to itself.
As a byproduct, we obtain that amenability of the Drinfeld double of a discrete quantum group
is equivalent to its strong amenability, which in turn implies that the discrete quantum group must be unimodular. Some of these facts seem to be known to experts, but we were unable
to locate precise references in the literature, and so we take the opportunity to use our techniques to give a self-contained account.

In addition we study the centre of the Fourier algebra for discrete quantum groups.
Here the situation is more subtle than for completely bounded multipliers.
We show that there is a canonical inclusion map from the biinvariant Fourier algebra of the Drinfeld double
to the central Fourier algebra, and prove that this map is an isomorphism for unimodular discrete quantum groups.
However, we also show that the biinvariant Fourier algebra may be strictly smaller than the central Fourier algebra in general. In fact, this happens already
for the dual of
\SU_{q}(2)
.

Let us explain how the paper is organised. In Section
2
we collect some background material and fix
our notation. Section
3
contains a brief review of completely bounded multipliers,
and in Section
4
we record the definitions of (strong) amenability, the Haagerup property, weak amenability, and the approximation property in
the setting of locally compact quantum groups and
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories.
In Section
5
we construct the averaging map for completely bounded multipliers of a locally compact quantum group with respect to a closed compact quantum subgroup.
In Section
6
we specialise to the setting of Drinfeld doubles of discrete quantum groups, and prove our main results concerning approximation properties.
Section
7
contains some further results on approximation properties for discrete quantum groups and their Drinfeld doubles, which are related to the underlying
discrete quantum group being unimodular. We show that central Fourier algebra elements can be approximated by central finitely-supported elements in this case, and that amenability of
the Drinfeld double of a discrete quantum group is equivalent to the discrete quantum group being amenable and unimodular.
Finally, in Section
8
we give equivalent characterisations for the biinvariant Fourier algebra of the Drinfeld double to agree with the central Fourier algebra
of a discrete quantum group. In addition we show that the centre of the Fourier algebra of any strongly amenable, non-unimodular, and centrally weakly amenable discrete quantum group is strictly larger than the biinvariant Fourier algebra of its Drinfeld double. This applies in particular to the dual of
\SU_{q}(2)
for
q\in\left]-1,1\right[\setminus\{0\}
.

We conclude with some general remarks on notation. When
\mathcal{A}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra we write
\M(\mathcal{A})
for its multiplier algebra. The canonical pairing between a Banach space
X
and its dual
X^{*}
will be denoted via
\langle\omega,x\rangle=\omega(x)
for
\omega\in X^{*},x\in X
. If
\mathscr{H}
is a Hilbert space we write
\omega_{\xi,\eta}
for the
vector functional
\omega_{\xi,\eta}(T)=(\xi|T\eta)
, and abbreviate
\omega_{\xi}=\omega_{\xi,\xi}
.
We write
\odot
for the algebraic tensor product,
\otimes
for the tensor product of Hilbert spaces or the minimal tensor product of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras, and
\M\bar{\otimes}\N

for the spatial tensor product of von Neumann algebras
\M,\N
. We denote by
\chi
the flip map for tensor products of algebras.

We would like to thank A. Skalski for several interesting comments on the first version of this paper.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we review some basic definitions and facts from the theory of locally compact quantum groups in the sense of Kustermans and Vaes
[
33
,
34
,
35
,
38
]
.

By definition, a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
is given by a von Neumann algebra
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
together with a normal unital
\star
-homomorphism
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
called
comultiplication
, satisfying
(\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
,
and
left
resp.
right

Haar integrals

\varphi
and
\psi
. These are normal, semifinite, faithful (n.s.f.) weights
on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
satisfying certain invariance conditions with respect to
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
. Note that in general the von Neumann algebra
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is non-commutative and will not be an algebra of function on a measure space. Following this notational convention, the predual of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is denoted by
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, and the GNS Hilbert space of
\varphi
by
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
.
We write
\Lambda_{\varphi}\colon\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
for the GNS map, where
\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}=\{x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,\varphi(x^{*}x)<\infty\}
.

With any locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
one can associate the
dual
locally compact quantum group
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
in such a way that the correspondence between
\mathbb{G}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
extends
Pontryagin duality. Furthermore, the Hilbert spaces
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
and
\LL^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
are identified in a canonical way.

The (left)
Kac-Takesaki operator

\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is the operator on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
defined via

((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x))\qquad(\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G}),x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}).

It is unitary and implements the comultiplication via
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x)=\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. By duality we also get the Kac-Takesaki operator for
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
, which
is linked to
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
via
\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=\chi(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})
. Tomita-Takesaki theory yields two groups of
modular automorphisms

(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}},(\sigma^{\psi}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

and
modular conjugations

J_{\varphi},J_{\psi}
associated with the weights
\varphi,\psi
, respectively.
We will also use the right Kac-Takesaki operator defined by

\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}}=(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\otimes J_{\widehat{\varphi}})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\otimes J_{\widehat{\varphi}})\in\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, where
J_{\widehat{\varphi}}
is the modular conjugation for the dual left Haar integral. The operator
\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}}
implements the comultiplication via
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x)=\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}}(x\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}*}
for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.

The
antipode

S_{\mathbb{G}}
is a densely defined, typically unbounded, operator on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
such that

(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{Dom}(S_{\mathbb{G}})\;\textnormal{ and }S_{\mathbb{G}}((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}\qquad(\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})),

and the
unitary antipode

R_{\mathbb{G}}
is a bounded, normal,
\star
-preserving, antimultiplicative map on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})

satisfying
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}R_{\mathbb{G}}=\chi(R_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes R_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
, and given by
R_{\mathbb{G}}(x)=J_{\varphi}x^{*}J_{\varphi}
for
x\in\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. These maps are linked via
S_{\mathbb{G}}=R_{\mathbb{G}}\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{-i/2}=\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{-i/2}R_{\mathbb{G}}
,
where
(\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is the group of
scaling automorphisms
of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.
The left and right Haar integrals are unique up to a scalar, and we will choose these scalars such that
\varphi=\psi\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}
.

We will mainly work with the weak
∗
-dense
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-subalgebra
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. It is defined as the norm-closure of
\{(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\,|\,\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\}
.
After restriction, the comultiplication becomes a non-degenerate
\star
-homomorphism
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
.
Similarly one defines
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, and then one obtains
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
.
Using the comultiplication of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, we define a Banach algebra structure on
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
via
\omega\star\nu=(\omega\otimes\nu)\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
for
\omega,\nu\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
.
As
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is the dual of
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, we have a canonical
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
-bimodule structure on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, given
by
\omega\star x=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x)
and
x\star\omega=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x)
.
Treating
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
as the predual of the von Neumann algebra
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
yields an
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
-bimodule structure on
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
defined
via
x\omega=\omega(\cdot\,x),\omega x=\omega(x\,\cdot)
for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
.

There is also a universal version of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
which we denote by
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})
, see
[
33
,
45
]
. It comes together with a comultiplication

\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\mathrm{C}^{u}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G}))
and a surjective
\star
-homomorphism
\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
which respects the comultiplications. The Kac-Takesaki operator
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
admits a lift to a unitary operator
\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))

satisfying
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}=\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
. Using this operator, we can introduce the half-lifted comultiplication

\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\ni x\mapsto\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\M(\mathrm{C}^{u}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})).

It satisfies
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}}=\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}
.
Similarly, writing
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})=J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})J_{\widehat{\varphi}}
, the right Kac-Takesaki operator
\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))

admits a lift to a unitary operator
\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G}))
satisfying
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}})\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}}=\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}}
. Recall from
[
35
, Section 4]
the opposite quantum group
\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}
, and that
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}}=\chi(\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}*})
. It follows that one way to define
\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}}
is as
\chi(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}*})
. We define

\Delta^{r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\ni x\mapsto\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}}(x\otimes\mathbbm{1})\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}*}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})),

and find that
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}}=\Delta^{r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}
.
Indeed, one could also define
\Delta^{r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}
using
\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}}
.
We can iterate these constructions, which we illustrate by way of an example. Define
\Delta^{u,r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}=(\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}
, and observe that

\displaystyle\Delta^{u,r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}=(\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}}

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta^{u}_{\mathbb{G}}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{r,u})(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{u}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Delta^{r,u}_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}.

As
\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}
is onto, this shows in particular that also
\Delta^{u,r,u}_{\mathbb{G}}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Delta^{r,u}_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta^{u,r}_{\mathbb{G}}
.

We define
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
by
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega)=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
, and similarly for
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. The
Fourier algebra of
\mathbb{G}
is
\A(\mathbb{G})=\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. One checks that
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
is multiplicative, and that
\A(\mathbb{G})
is a dense subalgebra of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. As
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
is also injective, we can
define an operator space structure on
\A(\mathbb{G})
by imposing the condition that
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\A(\mathbb{G})
is completely isometric.

We will also use two larger algebras: the
reduced Fourier-Stieltjes algebra

\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and the
Fourier-Stieltjes algebra

\B(\mathbb{G})
defined via

\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})=\{(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\,|\,\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}\},\quad\B(\mathbb{G})=\{(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega^{\prime})(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}*})\,|\,\omega^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}\}.

Together with the algebra structure of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and the norms
\|(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}=\|\omega\|
on
\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and
\|(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega^{\prime})(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}*})\|_{\B(\mathbb{G})}=\|\omega^{\prime}\|
on
\B(\mathbb{G})
, both
\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and
\B(\mathbb{G})
become Banach algebras. In fact, both are dual Banach algebras with preduals given
by
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, respectively. When speaking about the weak
∗
-topology on the (reduced) Fourier-Stieltjes algebra we mean the topology arising this
way. Furthermore we note that
\A(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\B(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
, and that the first two inclusions are isometric. We will sometimes write
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\omega)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}
also for
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
.

A locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
is called
compact
if
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
is unital, and in this case we write
\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{G})
for
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
.
If
\mathbb{G}
is compact then
\varphi=\psi
is a normal state, the
Haar state
, often denoted by
h\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
.
The representation theory of compact quantum groups shares many features with the one for classical compact groups.
In particular, every irreducible unitary representation of a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
is finite-dimensional, and every unitary representation decomposes into a direct sum
of irreducibles. We write
\Irr(\mathbb{G})
for the set of equivalence classes of irreducible unitary representations of
\mathbb{G}
,
and for each
\alpha\in\Irr(\mathbb{G})
let
U^{\alpha}=[U^{\alpha}_{i,j}]_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}\in\mathbb{M}_{\dim(\alpha)}(\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{G}))
be a representative. There is a unique positive invertible
matrix
\uprho_{\alpha}
with
\Tr(\uprho_{\alpha})=\Tr(\uprho_{\alpha}^{-1})
which intertwines
U^{\alpha}
and the double contragradient representation
(U^{\alpha})^{cc}
.
Let us also recall the Schur orthogonality relations

(2.1)

h\big((U^{\beta}_{i,k})^{*}U^{\alpha}_{j,l}\big)=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{k,l}\frac{(\uprho_{\alpha}^{-1})_{j,i}}{\Tr(\uprho_{\alpha})},\qquad h\big(U^{\alpha}_{i,j}(U^{\beta}_{k,l})^{*}\big)=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{i,k}\frac{(\uprho_{\alpha})_{l,j}}{\Tr(\uprho_{\alpha})}

for
\alpha,\beta\in\Irr(\mathbb{G}),1\leq i,j\leq\dim(\alpha),1\leq k,l\leq\dim(\beta)
. We often write
\dim_{q}(\alpha)=\Tr(\uprho_{\alpha})
the quantum dimension of
\alpha
.
Recall that the collection
\{U^{\alpha}_{i,j}\,|\,\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}),1\leq i,j\leq\dim(\alpha)\}
forms a basis for a dense Hopf
*
-subalgebra of
\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{G})
, which we denote by
\Pol(\mathbb{G})
. We have that
S(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=(U^{\alpha}_{j,i})^{*}
. The matrices
\uprho_{\alpha}
allow us to define functionals
f_{z}\colon\Pol(\mathbb{G})\to\mathbb{C}
by

f_{z}(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=(\rho_{\alpha}^{z})_{i,j}\qquad(z\in\mathbb{C}).

Then the scaling and modular automorphism groups are given by

\sigma_{z}=(f_{iz}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes f_{iz})\Delta^{(2)},\quad\tau_{z}=(f_{iz}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes f_{-iz})\Delta^{(2)}\qquad(z\in\mathbb{C}).

For
\alpha\in\Irr(\mathbb{G})
, the contragradient representation has matrix
[(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})^{*}]_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}
and is similar to the unitary representation
U^{\overline{\alpha}}
.

We say that

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

is
discrete
when
\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}
is compact, and write
\mathrm{c}_{0}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
for
\mathrm{C}_{0}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
in this case.
The representation theory of
\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}
implies that
\mathrm{c}_{0}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
is the
\mathrm{c}_{0}
-direct sum of finite-dimensional matrix algebras, and that
\ell^{\infty}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})=\LL^{\infty}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})

is the
\ell^{\infty}
-direct product. We write
\mathrm{c}_{00}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})\subseteq\mathrm{c}_{0}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
for the dense subalgebra of finitely-supported elements with respect to the direct
sum structure. We can write
\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}}\in\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})\bar{\otimes}\ell^{\infty}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
explicitly as

(2.2)

\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}U^{\alpha}_{i,j}\otimes e^{\alpha}_{i,j}

where
\{e^{\alpha}_{i,j}\}_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}
are the matrix units of the matrix block
\mathbb{M}_{\dim(\alpha)}(\mathbb{C})\subseteq\mathrm{c}_{0}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
.

If there is no risk of confusion we will sometimes omit subscripts and abbreviate e.g.
\Delta
for
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
and
\widehat{\Delta}
for
\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
.

3.
Completely bounded multipliers

In this section we recall some background on multipliers of locally compact quantum groups and
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories.
We follow the notation and conventions in
[
17
]
, and refer to
[
13
]
,
[
25
]
,
[
42
]
for more information.

A
left centraliser
of a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
is linear map
T\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
such that

T(\omega\star\omega^{\prime})=T(\omega)\star\omega^{\prime}\quad(\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})).

We denote by
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
the space of completely bounded left centralisers. Together with the completely bounded norm and composition,
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
becomes a Banach algebra.
We equip this space with an operator space structure by requiring that the embedding
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\hookrightarrow\CB(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is completely isometric.
This turns
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
into a completely contractive Banach algebra.

An operator
b\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is said to be a
completely bounded (CB) left multiplier
if
b\A(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\A(\mathbb{G})
and the associated map

\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\quad\text{satisfying}\quad b\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\widehat{\lambda}(\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}(\omega))\qquad(\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))

is completely bounded. Since
\widehat{\lambda}
is multiplicative we have
\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
for any
b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.
We write
\Theta^{l}(b)=(\Theta^{l}(b)_{*})^{*}
, and denote the space of CB left multipliers by
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Any Fourier algebra element
\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)\in\A(\mathbb{G})
is a CB left multiplier, with
\Theta^{l}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega))_{*}\in\CB(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
being the left multiplication by
\omega
and
\Theta^{l}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega))=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}
.
Let us also note that
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
.

If
T\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is a left centraliser then its Banach space dual
T^{*}
is a normal CB map on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
which is a left
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
-module homomorphism,
i.e.
T^{*}\in\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))}
. Then, by
[
26
, Corollary 4.4]
, there exists a unique CB left multiplier
b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))

satisfying
\Theta^{l}(b)=T^{*}
, that is,
\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}=T
. It follows that the map
\Theta^{l}(\cdot)_{*}\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\rightarrow C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is bijective. We define the operator space structure on
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
so that these spaces become completely isometric.

If
b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is such that
\Theta^{l}(b)
is completely positive, we say that
b
is a
completely positive (CP) left multiplier
. Furthermore,
b
is
normalised
if
\Theta^{l}(b)
is unital.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
a=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega})\in\A(\mathbb{G})
for some
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Then
a
is a CP multiplier if and only if
\widehat{\omega}
is positive, and

a
is normalised if and only if
\widehat{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})=1
.

Proof.

We know that
\Theta^{l}(a)=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}
and so
\Theta^{l}(a)(\mathbbm{1})=\mathbbm{1}
if and only if
\widehat{\omega}(\mathbbm{1})=1
. If
\widehat{\omega}
is positive then
\Theta^{l}(a)
is CP. Hence it
remains to prove that if
\Theta^{l}(a)
is CP then
\widehat{\omega}
is positive. By
[
13
, Theorem 5.2]
there is a positive linear functional

\mu\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
with
a=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\mu)(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{*})
. The Banach space adjoint
\Lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{*}\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}^{u}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
is isometric.
Let
\phi
be the composition of
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
with
\Lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{*}
, so
\phi
is also isometric. We see that

(\textnormal{id}\otimes\phi(\widehat{\omega}))(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{*})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{*})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})(\mathrm{W}^{*})=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega})=a=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\mu)(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{*}).

As
\{(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{*})\,|\,\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\}
is norm dense in
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, it follows that
\phi(\widehat{\omega})=\mu
, and so
\widehat{\omega}
is positive, as required.
∎

Since the inclusion
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\hookrightarrow\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is contractive, we can consider the restriction of the Banach space adjoint of this map,
giving a map
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}
. Let us define
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}
as the closure of the image of this map.
According to
[
25
, Theorem 3.4]
, the space
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is a predual of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, i.e. we have

Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}\cong\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))

completely isometrically. Whenever we speak about the weak
∗
-topology on
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
we will have in mind this particular choice of predual.
In this way
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
becomes a dual Banach algebra, that is, the multiplication of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is separately weak
∗
-continuous.

Next we recall the definition of multipliers on rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories from the work of Popa-Vaes
[
42
, Section 3]
.
If
\mathbf{T}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-category and
X,Y\in\mathbf{T}
are objects we write
\mathbf{T}(X,Y)
for the space of morphisms from
X
to
Y
.
We denote by
\textnormal{id}_{X}
or
id
the identity morphism in
\mathbf{T}(X,X)
.
By definition, a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor category is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-category
\mathbf{T}
together with a bilinear
*
-functor
\otimes\colon\mathbf{T}\times\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}
,
a distinguished object
1\!\!1\in\mathbf{T}
and unitary natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle 1\!\!1\otimes X\cong X\cong X\otimes 1\!\!1,\qquad(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\cong X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)

satisfying certain compatibility conditions. For simplicity we shall always assume that
\mathbf{T}
is
strict
, which means that these unitary natural isomorphisms are
identities, and that the tensor unit
1\!\!1
is simple.

We also assume that
\mathbf{T}
is
rigid
.
Every rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor category
\mathbf{T}
is semisimple, that is, every object of
\mathbf{T}
is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of simple objects.
We write
\Irr(\mathbf{T})
for the set of isomorphism classes of simple objects in
\mathbf{T}
, and choose representatives
X_{i}\in\mathbf{T}
for elements
i=[X_{i}]\in\Irr(\mathbf{T})
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be a rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor category. By definition, a
multiplier
on
\mathbf{T}
is a family
\theta=(\theta_{X,Y})
of linear
maps
\theta_{X,Y}\colon\mathbf{T}(X\otimes Y,X\otimes Y)\rightarrow\mathbf{T}(X\otimes Y,X\otimes Y)

for
X,Y\in\mathbf{T}
such that

\displaystyle\theta_{X_{2},Y_{2}}(gfh^{*})

\displaystyle=g\theta_{X_{1},Y_{1}}(f)h^{*},

\displaystyle\theta_{X_{2}\otimes X_{1},Y_{1}\otimes Y_{2}}(\textnormal{id}_{X_{2}}\otimes f\otimes\textnormal{id}_{Y_{2}})

\displaystyle=\textnormal{id}_{X_{2}}\otimes\theta_{X_{1},Y_{1}}(f)\otimes\textnormal{id}_{Y_{2}},

for all
X_{i},Y_{i}\in\mathbf{T}
,
f\in\mathbf{T}(X_{1}\otimes Y_{1},X_{1}\otimes Y_{1})
and
g,h\in\mathbf{T}(X_{1},X_{2})\otimes\mathbf{T}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\subseteq\mathbf{T}(X_{1}\otimes Y_{1},X_{2}\otimes Y_{2})
.
A multiplier
\theta=(\theta_{X,Y})
on
\mathbf{T}
is said to be completely positive (or a
CP multiplier
) if all the maps
\theta_{X,Y}
are completely positive.
A multiplier
\theta=(\theta_{X,Y})
on
\mathbf{T}
is said to be completely bounded (or a
CB multiplier
) if all the maps
\theta_{X,Y}
are completely bounded
and
\|\theta\|_{cb}=\sup_{X,Y\in\mathbf{T}}\|\theta_{X,Y}\|_{cb}<\infty
.

It is shown in
[
42
, Proposition 3.6]
that multipliers on
\mathbf{T}
are in canonical bijection with functions
\Irr(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and we will identify
a multiplier
\theta=(\theta_{X,Y})
with its associated function
\theta=(\theta(k))_{k\in\Irr(\mathbf{T})}
. Note that we have
\|(\theta(k))_{k\in\Irr(\mathbf{T})}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\theta\|_{cb}
.

We write
\M_{cb}(\mathbf{T})
for the space of CB multipliers on
\mathbf{T}
. Via composition of maps and the CB norm this becomes naturally a Banach algebra, such that the product on
\M_{cb}(\mathbf{T})
corresponds to pointwise multiplication of functions on
\Irr(\mathbf{T})
. In fact,
\M_{cb}(\mathbf{T})
is a dual Banach algebra, whose predual
Q(\mathbf{T})
can be constructed using
the tube algebra of
\mathbf{T}
, see
[
2
, Corollary 5.3]
.

A standard example of a rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor category is the category
\mathbf{T}=\Rep(\mathbb{G})
of finite dimensional unitary representations of a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
.
For a discrete quantum group

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

we shall write
\mathsf{Corep}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})=\Rep(\widehat{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}})
. Such
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories will be the only ones of interest to us in this paper.

4.
Approximation properties

In this section we review the definition of various approximation properties in the theory of locally compact quantum groups and rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories.

We begin with the case of locally compact quantum groups, compare
[
5
,
6
,
15
,
17
]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group. Then we say that
\mathbb{G}

•

is
strongly amenable
if there exists a bounded approximate identity of
\A(\mathbb{G})
consisting of CP multipliers of
\mathbb{G}
.

•

has the
Haagerup property
if there exists a bounded approximate identity of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
which consists of CP multipliers of
\mathbb{G}
.

•

is
weakly amenable
if there exists a left approximate identity
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\A(\mathbb{G})
satisfying
\lim\sup_{i\in I}\|e_{i}\|_{cb}<\infty
.
In this case, the smallest
M
such that we can choose
\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\leq M
for all
i\in I
is the
Cowling–Haagerup constant
of
\mathbb{G}
, denoted
\Lambda_{cb}(\mathbb{G})
.

•

has the
approximation property
if there exists a net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\A(\mathbb{G})
which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
topology of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.

While the definitions of the Haagerup property, weak amenability, and the approximation property for
\mathbb{G}
in Definition
4.1
are standard, this is not quite
the case for strong amenability. Our terminology here follows
[
19
]
,
[
48
]
. Usually, strong amenability of
\mathbb{G}

is phrased in terms of the dual, by equivalently saying that
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is
coamenable
.

A number of equivalent characterisations of coamenability can be found in
[
5
]
. In particular, conditions (6)–(8) in
[
5
, Theorem 3.1]

say that
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is coamenable if and only if
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\cong\A(\mathbb{G})
has a bounded (left, right, or two-sided) approximate identity. An examination of the proof (in particular,
that condition (5) implies conditions (6)–(8)) shows that when
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is coamenable, the Banach algebra
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
has a bounded (two-sided) approximate identity consisting of states;
compare also
[
24
, Theorem 2]
. That is,
\A(\mathbb{G})
has a bounded approximate identity consisting of CP multipliers.
It follows that our definition of strong amenability is compatible with
[
5
, Definition 3.1]
.
Since we can in fact choose the approximate identity to consist of states the associated multipliers will be normalised. Therefore we could equivalently strengthen the CP condition
in the definition of strong amenability to UCP.

Let us add that
amenability
of
\mathbb{G}
is defined in terms of the existence of an invariant mean on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, see Definition
7.6
. This is an a priori weaker notion than strong amenability,
and the two concepts are known to be equivalent only in special cases, compare
[
6
]
,
[
48
]
.

For equivalent characterisations of the Haagerup property see
[
15
, Theorem 6.5]
; however be aware that some equivalent conditions in this reference may require a second
countability assumption.

In the setting of discrete quantum groups one also considers central approximation properties. We recall that a multiplier
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})

is called central if it is contained in the centre of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, or equivalently, in the centre of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

be a discrete quantum group. Then we say that

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

•

is
centrally strongly amenable
if there is a net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
consisting of finitely supported central CP multipliers of

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

converging to
\mathbbm{1}
pointwise.

•

has the
central Haagerup property
if there exists a bounded approximate identity of
\mathrm{c}_{0}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
which consists of central CP multipliers of

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

.

•

is
centrally weakly amenable
if there exists a net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of finitely supported central multipliers converging to
\mathbbm{1}
pointwise and
satisfying
\lim\sup_{i\in I}\|e_{i}\|_{cb}<\infty
.
In this case, the smallest
M
such that we can choose
\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\leq M
for all
i\in I
is the
central Cowling–Haagerup constant
of

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

,
denoted
\mathcal{Z}\Lambda_{cb}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
.

•

has the
central approximation property
if there exists a net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of finitely supported central multipliers which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
topology
of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.

Note that
\mathrm{c}_{00}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})\subseteq\A({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
is a dense subspace for the Fourier algebra norm. It follows that a bounded approximate identity of
\A({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
is the
same thing as a bounded net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\A({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
converging to
\mathbbm{1}
pointwise. If

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

is strongly amenable then there is such a net which consists of finitely
supported CP multipliers, as can be seen by suitably approximating the associated states in
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Thus central strong amenability is a natural central version of our definition of strong amenability. However, we do not know whether the a priori weaker requirement of existence of a net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of CP multipliers in the centre of
\A({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
converging to
\mathbbm{1}
pointwise is equivalent to central strong amenability; though this is true when

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

is unimodular, see Proposition
7.4
below.

Finally, let us review the corresponding notions for rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories
[
42
]
,
[
17
]
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be a rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor category. Then
\mathbf{T}

•

is
amenable
if there exists a net
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}
of finitely supported CP multipliers of
\mathbf{T}
converging to
\mathbbm{1}
pointwise.

•

has the
Haagerup property
if there exists a net of CP multipliers
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathbf{T}
converging to
\mathbbm{1}
pointwise such that
\varphi_{i}\in\mathrm{c}_{0}(\Irr(\mathbf{T}))

for all
i\in I
.

•

is
weakly amenable
if there exists a net of finitely supported CB multipliers
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathbf{T}
converging to
\mathbbm{1}
pointwise
such that
\lim\sup_{i\in I}\|\varphi_{i}\|_{cb}<\infty
. In this case, the smallest constant
M
such that we can choose
\|\varphi_{i}\|_{cb}\leq M
for all
i\in I
is
the
Cowling–Haagerup constant
of
\mathbf{T}
, denoted
\Lambda_{cb}(\mathbf{T})
.

•

has the
approximation property
if there exists a net of finitely supported CB multipliers of
\mathbf{T}
converging to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak*-topology of
\M_{cb}(\mathbf{T})
.

If

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

is a discrete quantum group then central multipliers for

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

and categorical multipliers for
\mathsf{Corep}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
can
both be viewed as functions on
\Irr(\widehat{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})=\Irr(\mathsf{Corep}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}))
.
It turns out that, in this way, the space of central CB-multipliers of

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

identifies isometrically with the space of CB multipliers of
\mathsf{Corep}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
.
Moreover the weak
∗
-topologies agree, and this identification restricts to a bijection between the corresponding CP-multipliers

[
42
, Proposition 6.1]
,
[
17
, Lemma 8.6]
. By definition, finite support, being
\mathrm{c}_{0}
and pointwise convergence of multipliers
have the same meaning in either case.

As a consequence, the categorical approximation properties for the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor category
\mathsf{Corep}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}})
in Definition
4.3
are equivalent to the
corresponding central approximation properties for

\textstyle\mathbb{L}

in Definition
4.2
.

5.
The averaging construction

In this section we discuss the averaging construction for multipliers on a locally compact quantum group with respect to a compact quantum subgroup. Let us note that averaging with
respect to compact quantum subgroups has been widely used previously in different contexts, most notably in the study of quantum homogeneous spaces and compact quantum hypergroups,
see for instance
[
8
]
,
[
11
]
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group and let
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{G}
be a compact quantum subgroup
[
16
, Section 6.1]
. This means that there is a
nondegenerate
\star
-homomorphism
\pi\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}^{u}(\mathbb{K})\xrightarrow{\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}}\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{K})
, compatible with comultiplications, such that the dual
nondegenerate
\star
-homomorphism
\mathrm{c}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{K}})\rightarrow\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
drops to a normal injective
unital
\star
-homomorphism
\widehat{\pi}\colon\ell^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{K}})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
.

The morphisms
\pi
and
\widehat{\pi}
can also be encoded at the level of the Kac-Takesaki operators, see for instance
[
37
]
.
If
\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is the half-universal lift of
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
then we obtain a
bicharacter
(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})\in\M(\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{K})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
and a left action
\lambda_{\pi}\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M(\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{K})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
given by

\lambda_{\pi}(a)=(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})^{*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}).

This is the restriction, to
\mathbb{K}
, of the left translation action of
\mathbb{G}
on itself, and extends to an action
\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})\bar{\otimes}\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})

on the level of von Neumann algebras. In a similar way, using the right Kac-Takesaki operator, one obtains a right action
\rho_{\pi}\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{K}))

and the corresponding normal extension
\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
.

Combining
\lambda_{\pi}
and
\rho_{\pi}
with the Haar state
h
of
\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
we define the
averaging map

\Xi\colon\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
by

(5.1)

\Xi=(h\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h)(\textnormal{id}\otimes\rho_{\pi})\lambda_{\pi}=(h\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h)(\lambda_{\pi}\otimes\textnormal{id})\rho_{\pi}.

Clearly
\Xi
is a normal unital contractive CP map.

This construction behaves well on the level of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras. For the following, recall from Section
2
the maps
\Delta^{r,u},\Delta^{u,r}
and their iterated counterparts.

Lemma 5.1
.

The averaging map
\Xi
restricts to a contractive CP map
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
such that

(5.2)

\Xi(a)=(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)\qquad(a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})).

It also restricts to a strictly continuous unital contractive CP map
\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))\rightarrow\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
, given by the same formula.

Proof.

For
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pi}(a)

\displaystyle=(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})^{*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})

\displaystyle=(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(a).

Similarly,
\rho_{\pi}(a)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)
. Thus for
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
,

\Xi(a)=(h\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h)((\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)=(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a),

as claimed.

From
[
33
, Proposition 6.1]
we know that
(\Delta^{u}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23}
. Thus

\displaystyle(\Delta^{u,r}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{u,r}\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\Delta^{u}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23})=\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23}.

Hence, given
a=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\in\A(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
for some
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, we see that

\displaystyle(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(a)

\displaystyle=(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{23}\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{13})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\big(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(1\otimes(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}))\big).

This equals
(\textnormal{id}\otimes b\omega)(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\in\A(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
for
b=(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. By density, we conclude that
(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}
maps
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
to itself.

Analogously,
(\Delta^{r,u}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23}
, and so

(\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi\otimes\omega)(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{23}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{13})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega b)(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\in\A(\mathbb{G}),

so also
(\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{r,u}
maps
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
to itself. By composition,
\Xi(a)\in\A(\mathbb{G})
and
\Xi
maps
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
to itself.

By
[
33
, Proposition 6.1]
we know that
\Delta^{u}
is a non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism, and hence the same is true of
\Delta^{u,r,u}
. As
\pi
is non-degenerate, it follows that
(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}
is non-degenerate, hence strictly continuous. The strict extension to
\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
hence agrees with
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\rho_{\pi})\lambda_{\pi}
when restricted from
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
to
\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
. As
h
is strictly continuous, and slice maps are strictly continuous,
[
36
, Proposition 8.3]
, it follows
that
\Xi\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
is strictly continuous, and again, the strict extension to
\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
agrees with the
restriction of
\Xi\colon\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

The structure of this proof shows that we could analogously define one-sided averaging maps
\Xi^{l}\colon\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),\Xi^{l}(a)=(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(a)

and
\Xi^{r}\colon\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),\Xi^{r}(a)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)
. These maps
\Xi^{l},\Xi^{r}
are both contractive, CP and map
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
to itself.

From now on we will freely view
\Xi
as an endomorphism of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
or
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
, but for the sake of clarity we shall sometimes indicate
which version we are using. We remark that the proof of Lemma
5.1
shows that
\Xi
maps
\A(\mathbb{G})
to itself; see Lemma
5.5
below for a different
way to verify this.

Let us write
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})\subseteq\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
for the image of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
under
\Xi
.

Lemma 5.3
.

The following hold:

•

\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\mid(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)=\mathbbm{1}\otimes a\otimes\mathbbm{1}\}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\mid(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(a)=\mathbbm{1}\otimes a,(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)=a\otimes\mathbbm{1}\}.

•

The map
\Xi\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
is a conditional expectation onto
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
so that
\Xi(a)\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
. Then

\displaystyle(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(\Xi(a))

\displaystyle=(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}\big((h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)\big)

\displaystyle=(h\pi\otimes\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,u,r,u}(a)=((h\pi\otimes\pi)\Delta^{u}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)

\displaystyle=((h\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{K}}\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\otimes(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)=\mathbbm{1}\otimes\Xi(a).

An entirely analogous calculation shows that
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(\Xi(a))=\Xi(a)\otimes\mathbbm{1}
. Conversely, let
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
be such that
(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(a)=\mathbbm{1}\otimes a
and
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)=a\otimes\mathbbm{1}
. Then

(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)=((\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}\otimes\textnormal{id})\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)\big)=((\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}\otimes\textnormal{id})(a\otimes\mathbbm{1})=\mathbbm{1}\otimes a\otimes\mathbbm{1}.

Now suppose that
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
with
(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)=\mathbbm{1}\otimes a\otimes\mathbbm{1}
. Then

a=(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)=\Xi(a).

We have shown the stated forms for elements of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
. It follows easily that
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-subalgebra.

It is clear from construction that
\Xi
is a contraction. For
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
we obtain, using the above obtained description,

\Xi(a)=(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{u,r,u}(a)=(h\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes h)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a\otimes\mathbbm{1})=a.

Hence
\Xi
is a contractive projection. From Tomiyama’s theorem,
[
7
, Theorem 1.5.10]
, it follows that
\Xi
is a conditional expectation.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

We could analogously define
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G})
to be the image of
(h\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}
, and then the same argument shows that

\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G})=\{a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(a)=\mathbbm{1}\otimes a\}.

Similarly, denote by
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
the image of
(\textnormal{id}\otimes h\pi)\Delta^{r,u}
, and then

\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})=\{a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta^{r,u}(a)=a\otimes\mathbbm{1}\}.

Hence we have shown that
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})=\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G})\cap\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})
.

We now discuss some properties of slicing with functionals defined at the universal level. Given
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
and
a\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
we write

\omega\star a=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta^{r,u}(a),\quad a\star\omega=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(a).

A priori both
\omega\star a
and
a\star\omega
belong to the multiplier algebra
\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
, but as shown in Lemma
5.5
below,
we have in fact
\omega\star a,a\star\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
for any
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
. These operations turn
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
and
\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
into a
bimodule over
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
.

By taking the Banach space dual of the map
\pi\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{K})
, we obtain a contractive algebra
homomorphism
\pi^{*}\colon\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{K})^{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
, with respect to the convolution product. In particular,
\pi^{*}(h)
is an idempotent state in
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
. Notice that the averaging map
\Xi
can be written as
\Xi(a)=\pi^{*}(h)\star a\star\pi^{*}(h)
for
a\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
.

It is straightforward to check that
\A(\mathbb{G})
and
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
are contractive left and right
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
-modules, compare
[
17
, Proposition 4.5]
, a result we now extend to the Fourier–Stieltjes algebras as defined in Section
2
. As preparation, notice that the Banach space dual map
\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}^{*}\colon\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})^{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
is an isometric algebra homomorphism since
\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}
is a quotient map which intertwines the coproducts. Also recall that the canonical map
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
is an isometric algebra homomorphism which identifies
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
as an ideal in
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
, see the discussion after
[
34
, Proposition 8.3]
. The composition of these maps gives a canonical isometric homomorphism
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
, whose image is again an ideal, see for example
[
12
, Proposition 8.3]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

The following hold:

1)

Let
a\in\A(\mathbb{G})
and
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
. Then
a\star\omega\in\A(\mathbb{G})
and
\|a\star\omega\|_{\A(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|a\|_{\A(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|
. Similarly,
\omega\star a\in\A(\mathbb{G})

and
\|\omega\star a\|_{\A(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|a\|_{\A(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|
.

2)

Let
b\in\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
. Then
b\star\omega\in\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and
\|b\star\omega\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|b\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|
. Similarly,
\omega\star b\in\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})

and
\|\omega\star b\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|b\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|
.

3)

Let
b\in\B(\mathbb{G})
and
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
. Then
b\star\omega\in\B(\mathbb{G})
and
\|b\star\omega\|_{\B(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|b\|_{\B(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|
. Similarly,
\omega\star b\in\B(\mathbb{G})

and
\|\omega\star b\|_{\B(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|b\|_{\B(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|
.

4)

Let
c\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
and
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
. Then
c\star\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
and
\|c\star\omega\|\leq\|c\|\|\omega\|
. Similarly,
\omega\star c\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})

and
\|\omega\star c\|\leq\|c\|\|\omega\|
.

Proof.

2)
Write
b=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega})\in\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
for
\widehat{\omega}\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
. Then

(5.3)

\begin{split}b\star\omega&=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}\bigl((\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id}){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{13}{\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{12}\bigr)\\
&=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\bigr)=\widehat{\lambda}\bigl(\widehat{\omega}\bigl(\cdot\,(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)\bigr)\in\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})\end{split}

as required. We also immediately get the bound on the
\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
-norm of
b\star\omega
:

\|b\star\omega\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}=\bigl\|\widehat{\omega}\bigl(\cdot\,(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)\bigr\|\leq\|\widehat{\omega}\|\|\omega\|=\|b\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|.

Similarly, we get

(5.4)

\begin{split}\omega\star b&=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta^{r,u}\bigl((\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id}){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\bigl({\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{13}{\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{12}\bigr)\\
&=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl(((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)=\widehat{\lambda}\bigl(\widehat{\omega}\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\;\cdot\bigr)\bigr)\in\B_{r}(\mathbb{G}),\end{split}

and
\|\omega\star b\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|\omega\|\|b\|_{\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})}
. This proves the second point.

1)
If
a=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega})
for
\widehat{\omega}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\subseteq\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
, then since
\widehat{\omega}\bigl(\cdot\,(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)
and
\widehat{\omega}\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\,\cdot\bigr)
are normal functionals, the above reasoning proves also the first point.

3)
This is completely analogous: if
b=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}*})\in\B(\mathbb{G})
with
\widehat{\omega}\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
, then

(5.5)

\begin{split}b\star\omega&=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}\bigl((\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id}){\mathds{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl({\mathds{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{13}{{\mathds{V}\!\!\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{12}\bigr)\\
&=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl({\mathds{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega){\mathds{V}\!\!\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\bigr)=\bigl(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}\bigl(\cdot\,(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega){\mathds{V}\!\!\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{V}$}}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)\bigr)(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\mathbb{G}*})\in\B(\mathbb{G})\end{split}

and
\|b\star\omega\|_{\B(\mathbb{G})}\leq\|b\|_{\B(\mathbb{G})}\|\omega\|
. The argument for
\omega\star b
is similar.

4)
Since
\A(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
is norm dense, we can approximate
c
by a sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements from the Fourier algebra
\A(\mathbb{G})
. As the map
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\ni c^{\prime}\mapsto c^{\prime}\star\omega\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
is continuous, we obtain

c\star\omega=(\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n})\star\omega=\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}\star\omega\in\overline{\A(\mathbb{G})}=\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}).

The norm estimate follows as
\|c\star\omega\|=\|(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta^{r,u}(c)\|\leq\|\omega\|\|\Delta^{r,u}(c)\|\leq\|\omega\|\|c\|
. Again, the argument for
\omega\star c
is entirely analogous.
∎

Corollary 5.6
.

The averaging map
\Xi
restricts to contractive linear maps
\A(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\A(\mathbb{G})
,
\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and
\B(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\B(\mathbb{G})
.
Furthermore, the maps
\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and
\B(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\B(\mathbb{G})
are weak
∗
-weak
∗
-continuous.

Proof.

As
\Xi(a)=\pi^{*}(h)\star a\star\pi^{*}(h)
, and
\|\pi^{*}(h)\|=\|h\|=1
, this follows immediately from Lemma
5.5
together with equations (
5.3
), (
5.4
), (
5.5
) whose form shows weak
∗
-weak
∗
-continuity of
\Xi
.
∎

We write
\A(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})\subseteq\A(\mathbb{G})
(resp.
\B_{r}(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})\subseteq\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
and
\B(\mathbb{K}\backslash\mathbb{G}/\mathbb{K})\subseteq\B(\mathbb{G})
) for the image of
\A(\mathbb{G})
(resp.
\B_{r}(\mathbb{G})
,
\B(\mathbb{G})
) under
\Xi
.

As
\A(\mathbb{G})\cong\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, the averaging map induces a map
\Xi_{1}\colon\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, and taking the Banach space adjoint, a normal map
\Xi_{\infty}\colon\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. We now explore what these maps are. Let
p_{e}\in\ell^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{K}})
denote the central projection onto the matrix factor corresponding to the trivial representation
e\in\Irr(\mathbb{K})
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The maps
\Xi_{1}\colon\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
\Xi_{\infty}\colon\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
are given by

\Xi_{1}(\widehat{\omega})=\widehat{\omega}(\widehat{\pi}(p_{e})\cdot\widehat{\pi}(p_{e}))\quad(\widehat{\omega}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})),\qquad\Xi_{\infty}(\widehat{x})=\widehat{\pi}(p_{e})\widehat{x}\widehat{\pi}(p_{e})\quad(\widehat{x}\in\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})),

respectively.

Proof.

As in the proof of Corollary
5.6
, we know that for
a\in\A(\mathbb{G})
we have that
\Xi(a)=\pi^{*}(h)\star a\star\pi^{*}(h)
. Then, given
a=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega})
for some
\widehat{\omega}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, by the proof of Lemma
5.5
we know that

\pi^{*}(h)\star a\star\pi^{*}(h)=\widehat{\lambda}\big(\widehat{\omega}\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi^{*}(h))\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\cdot(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi^{*}(h))\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\big)\big).

As
(\pi\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\pi})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{K}})
, equivalently,
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi)(\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})=(\widehat{\pi}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{K}}})
, it follows that
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\pi^{*}(h))\text{\reflectbox{$\mathds{W}$}}\>\!^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=\widehat{\pi}\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes h)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{K}}}\big)=\widehat{\pi}(p_{e})
. Hence

\Xi(\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}))=\Xi(a)=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}(\widehat{\pi}(p_{e})\cdot\widehat{\pi}(p_{e}))),

and the stated formula for
\Xi_{1}
follows. The formula for
\Xi_{\infty}
follows by direct calculation.
∎

Let us next discuss the compatibility of
\Xi
with CB multipliers.
As usual, given
\omega\in\mathrm{C}^{u}_{0}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
we denote by
\overline{\omega}
the functional defined by
\overline{\omega}(x)=\overline{\omega(x^{*})}
for
x\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})
.
Recall from
[
33
, Section 7.9]
that the scaling automorphism group and the (unitary) antipode admit lifts to the universal level
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})
,
which we denote by
(\tau_{t}^{u})_{t\in\mathbb{R}},R_{u}
, and
S_{u}
, respectively. In the same way as at the reduced level, they are connected via the formula
S_{u}=R_{u}\tau^{u}_{-i/2}
.

Whenever
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
is such that the map
D(S_{u})\ni a\mapsto\overline{\langle\omega,S_{u}(a)^{*}\rangle}\in\mathbb{C}
extends to a bounded functional on
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})
, we denote this extension by
\omega^{\sharp}
. This is in agreement with the usual definition
of
\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, see
[
35
, Definition 2.3]
.

Lemma 5.8
.

We have
\overline{\pi^{*}(h)}=\pi^{*}(h)=\pi^{*}(h)^{\sharp}
.

Proof.

The first equality is immediate since
\pi
is a
\star
-homomorphism and
h
is a state. For the second, recall that
\pi
commutes with the
antipode, see
[
37
, Proposition 3.10]
, and
hS_{\mathbb{K}}=h
on
D(S_{\mathbb{K}})
.
∎

We wish to study convolution of multipliers by elements of
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
. To avoid developing some theory around applying completely bounded maps to multiplier algebras, we shall use von Neumann algebra techniques. By
[
40
, Proposition 3.12.3]
, for example, for a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
contained in a von Neumann algebra
\M
we can identify
\M(\mathcal{A})
with
\{x\in\M\,|\,xa,ax\in\mathcal{A}\ (a\in\mathcal{A})\}
. In particular, we will view the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
as being contained in the von
Neumann algebra
\mathrm{C}^{u}_{0}(\mathbb{G})^{**}\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, and so consider
\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
as an element of
\mathrm{C}^{u}_{0}(\mathbb{G})^{**}\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
a\in\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
and
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
.

1)

We have
a\star\omega\in\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
and
\|a\star\omega\|_{cb}\leq\|a\|_{cb}\|\omega\|
. Furthermore
\Theta^{l}(a\star\omega)
is the map

(5.6)

\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\ni\widehat{x}\mapsto(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}).

2)

Take
\omega\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
and assume that
\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}
exists. Then
\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star a\in\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))

and
\|\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star a\|_{cb}\leq\|\omega\|\|a\|_{cb}
.
Furthermore,
\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star a=S^{-1}(S^{-1}(a)^{*}\star\omega)^{*}
and
\Theta^{l}(\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star a)
is the map

(5.7)

\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\ni\widehat{x}\mapsto(\overline{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))(\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x}))\bigr)\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}).

Proof.

1)
This claim is analogous to
[
17
, Proposition 4.5]
. The functional
\omega
may be regarded as a normal functional on
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{u}(\mathbb{G})^{**}
.
Therefore, given the discussion above, it makes sense to define

T\colon\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\ni\widehat{x}\mapsto(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}).

Observe that
\|T\|_{cb}\leq\|a\|_{cb}\|\omega\|
. Now one can proceed exactly as in
[
17
, Proposition 4.5]
to show that
T
is a module map on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and that the associated multiplier is
a\star\omega\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with
\Theta^{l}(a\star\omega)=T
.

2)
It follows from
[
17
, Lemma 4.8, Proposition 4.9]
that
a\in D(S^{-1})
and that
b=S^{-1}(a)^{*}
is contained in
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with
\|b\|_{cb}=\|a\|_{cb}
. The previous point gives
b\star\omega\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, hence also
b\star\omega\in D(S^{-1})
. We claim that

S^{-1}(b\star\omega)=\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\star S^{-1}(b),

compare
[
14
, Lemma 4.11]
.
Indeed, using
[
33
, Propositions 7.2, 9.2]
one easily checks that
\chi(R_{u}\otimes R)\Delta^{u,r}=\Delta^{u,r}R
and
(\tau^{u}_{t}\otimes\tau_{t})\Delta^{u,r}=\Delta^{u,r}\tau_{t}\,(t\in\mathbb{R})
. Then

\begin{split}&\quad\;S^{-1}(b\star\omega)=R\tau_{i/2}\bigl((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(b)\bigr)=R\bigl((\omega\tau^{u}_{-i/2}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta^{u,r}(\tau_{i/2}(b))\bigr)\\
&=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega\tau^{u}_{-i/2}R^{u})\Delta^{r,u}(R\tau_{i/2}(b))=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\overline{\omega}^{\sharp})\Delta^{r,u}(S^{-1}(b))=\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\star S^{-1}(b).\end{split}

In the above calculation we used the fact that
\omega S_{u}=\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}
on
D(S_{u})
, in particular
\omega\tau^{u}_{-i/2}
is bounded. Using again the previous point and
[
17
, Lemma 4.8, Proposition 4.9]
we conclude that

\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star a=\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star S^{-1}(b)^{*}=\bigl(\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\star S^{-1}(b)\bigr)^{*}=S^{-1}(b\star\omega)^{*}\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G})).

Furthermore, we now see that

\big\|\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star a\big\|_{cb}=\big\|S^{-1}(b\star\omega)^{*}\big\|_{cb}=\big\|b\star\omega\big\|_{cb}\leq\|b\|_{cb}\|\omega\|=\|a\|_{cb}\|\omega\|,

as claimed. Finally, by
[
17
, Proposition 4.9]
we know that
\Theta^{l}(b)=\Theta^{l}(S(a^{*}))=\Theta^{l}(a)^{\dagger}
. Thus, for
\widehat{x}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
,

\displaystyle\Theta^{l}(\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\star a)(\widehat{x})

\displaystyle=\Theta^{l}\big(S^{-1}(b\star\omega)^{*}\big)(\widehat{x})=\Theta^{l}\big(b\star\omega\big)(\widehat{x}^{*})^{*}

\displaystyle=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(b))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x}^{*})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathds{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)^{*},

using (
5.6
). This is then equal to
